\begin{lemma}\label{real_consecutive_integers_nonneg}
    Suppose $x\in\reals$ and $0 \leq x$.
    Then there exists $n\in\integers$ such that $n \leq x$ and $x < n + 1$ and either $n = 0$ or $n\in\naturalsPlus$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    Let $S = \{ n\in\naturalsPlus \mid x < n\}$.
    Take $N\in\naturalsPlus$ such that $x < N$ by \cref{real_archimedean_property}.
    Thus $S\neq \emptyset$.
    Take $m\in S$ such that for all $a\in S$ we have $m \leq a$ by \cref{subseteq,real_naturals_least_element}.
    Thus $x < m$.
    \begin{byCase}
    \caseOf{$m = 1$.}
        Take $n$ such that $n = 0$.
        $n \in \reals$.
        $n\in\integers$ by \cref{real_integers}.
        Thus $x < n + 1$ by \cref{real_one_in_naturals,real_naturals_subset_reals,subseteq,real_add_ident}.
        Hence there exists $k\in\integers$ such that $k \leq x$ and $x < k + 1$ and either $k = 0$ or $k\in\naturalsPlus$.
    \caseOf{$m \neq 1$.}
        Take $p\in\naturalsPlus$ such that $m = p + 1$ by \cref{real_naturals_predecessor}.
        Thus it is wrong that $x < p$ by \cref{real_naturals_subset_reals,real_one_in_naturals,subseteq,real_add_ident,real_zero_lt_one,real_lt_add,real_add_comm,real_lt_trans,real_leq_antisym,real_lt_irreflexive}.
        Take $n$ such that $n = p$.
        $n \in \reals$.
        $n\in\integers$ by \cref{real_integers}.
        Thus $n \leq x$ by \cref{real_naturals_subset_reals,subseteq,real_lt_trichotomy}.
        Therefore there exists $k\in\integers$ such that $k \leq x$ and $x < k + 1$ and either $k = 0$ or $k\in\naturalsPlus$.
    \end{byCase}
\end{proof}

% consecutive integers
\begin{proposition}\label{real_consecutive_integers}
    Suppose $x\in\reals$.
    Then there exists $n\in\integers$ such that $n \leq x$ and $x < n + 1$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    \begin{byCase}
    \caseOf{$0 \leq x$.}
        Follows by \cref{real_consecutive_integers_nonneg}.
    \caseOf{$x < 0$.}
        Let $y = \neg{x}$.
        $y\in\reals$ by \cref{real_add_inverse}.
        Thus $0 < y$ by \cref{real_add_ident,real_add_inverse_unique,real_lt_neg}.
        Take $t$ such that $t\in\integers$ and $t \leq y$ and $y < t + 1$ and either $t = 0$ or $t\in\naturalsPlus$ by \cref{real_consecutive_integers_nonneg}.
        Hence $t\in\reals$ by \cref{real_add_ident,real_naturals_subset_reals,subseteq}.
        Show that $t + 1\in\naturalsPlus$.
        \begin{subproof}
            \begin{byCase}
            \caseOf{$t = 0$.}
                $1\in\naturalsPlus$ by \cref{real_one_in_naturals}.
                Thus $t + 1\in\naturalsPlus$.
            \caseOf{$t \neq 0$.}
                $t + 1\in\naturalsPlus$ by \cref{real_naturals_closed_succ}.
            \end{byCase}
        \end{subproof}
        Let $k = \neg{(t + 1)}$.
        $k\in\reals$.
        Thus $k\in\integers$. % by \cref{real_naturals_subset_reals,subseteq,real_add_inverse,real_add_comm,real_integers}.
        Hence $k + 1 = \neg{t}$ by \cref{real_naturals_subset_reals,real_one_in_naturals,subseteq,real_neg_addition,real_add_inverse,real_add_assoc,real_add_comm,real_add_ident}.
        \begin{byCase}
        \caseOf{$x = k + 1$.}
            There exists $n\in\integers$ such that $n \leq x$ and $x < n + 1$ by \cref{real_add_inverse,real_add_comm,real_integers,real_add_ident,real_naturals_subset_reals,real_one_in_naturals,subseteq,real_zero_lt_one,real_lt_add}.
        \caseOf{$x \neq k + 1$.}
            There exists $n\in\integers$ such that $n \leq x$ and $x < n + 1$ by \cref{real_add_inverse,real_leq_neg,real_neg_neg,real_naturals_subset_reals,real_one_in_naturals,subseteq,real_add_closed,real_lt_trichotomy}.
        \end{byCase}
    \end{byCase}
\end{proof}
  
% helper lemma 3: rationals in nonnegative intervals
\begin{lemma}\label{real_rationals_nonneg_interval}
    Suppose $a\in\reals$ and $b\in\reals$ and $0 \leq a$ and $a < b$.
    Then there exist $n, N$ such that $n\in\naturalsPlus$ and $N\in\naturalsPlus$ and $n / N\in\intervalopen{a}{b}$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    $0 < b - a$ by \cref{real_lt_imp_pos_diff}.
    Take $N\in\naturalsPlus$ such that $0 < 1 / N$ and $1 / N < b - a$ by \cref{real_add_inverse,real_sub,real_add_closed,real_small_reciprocal}.
    Thus $0 < N$ by \cref{real_naturals_subset_reals,subseteq,real_mul_ident,real_add_ident,real_zero_lt_one,real_one_leq_naturalsplus,real_lt_trans}.
    Hence $N\neq 0$ by \cref{real_naturals_subset_reals,subseteq,real_pos_nonzero}.
    Therefore $0 < \inv{N}$ by \cref{real_naturals_subset_reals,subseteq,real_mul_inverse,real_inv_pos}.
    Let $S = \{ n\in\naturalsPlus \mid a < n / N\}$.
    Take $n\in\naturalsPlus$ such that $a \rmul N < n$ by \cref{real_naturals_subset_reals,subseteq,real_mul_closed,real_archimedean_property}.
    $(a \rmul N) \rmul \inv{N} < n \rmul \inv{N}$ by \cref{real_mul_inverse,real_mul_closed,real_naturals_subset_reals,subseteq,real_lt_mul_pos}.
    Thus $a < n / N$ by \cref{real_naturals_subset_reals,subseteq,real_mul_assoc,real_mul_inverse,real_mul_comm,real_mul_ident,real_div}.
    Hence $S\neq \emptyset$.
    Take $k\in S$ such that for all $m\in S$ we have $k \leq m$ by \cref{subseteq,real_naturals_least_element}.
    Let $p = k / N$.
    We have $p\in\reals$ by \cref{real_naturals_subset_reals,subseteq,real_mul_inverse,real_div,real_mul_closed}.
    We have $a + 1 / N < b$ by \cref{real_naturals_subset_reals,subseteq,real_mul_inverse,real_mul_ident,real_div,real_mul_closed,real_sub,real_add_closed,real_lt_add,real_add_comm,real_add_assoc,real_add_inverse,real_add_ident}.
    Show that $p < b$.
    \begin{subproof}
        \begin{byCase}
        \caseOf{$k = 1$.}
            We have $p < b$ by \cref{real_naturals_subset_reals,subseteq,real_mul_inverse,real_mul_ident,real_div,real_mul_closed,real_add_closed,real_lt_trichotomy,real_lt_add,real_add_ident,real_lt_trans}.
        \caseOf{$k \neq 1$.}
            Take $m\in\naturalsPlus$ such that $k = m + 1$ by \cref{real_naturals_predecessor}.
            Show that it is wrong that $a < m / N$.
            \begin{subproof}
                Suppose not.
                Thus $k \neq m + 1$.
                Contradiction.
            \end{subproof}
            \begin{byCase}
            \caseOf{$m / N = a$.}
                We have $p < b$ by \cref{real_naturals_subset_reals,subseteq,real_mul_inverse,real_div,real_right_distrib,real_mul_ident}.
            \caseOf{$m / N \neq a$.}
                We have $p < b$ by \cref{real_naturals_subset_reals,subseteq,real_mul_inverse,real_mul_ident,real_div,real_right_distrib,real_mul_closed,real_add_closed,real_lt_trichotomy,real_lt_add,real_lt_trans}.
            \end{byCase}
        \end{byCase}
    \end{subproof}
    Thus there exist $i, j$ such that $i\in\naturalsPlus$ and $j\in\naturalsPlus$ and $i / j\in\intervalopen{a}{b}$ by \cref{subseteq,intervalopen}.
\end{proof}

% rational in an open interval
\begin{proposition}\label{real_rational_open_interval}
    Suppose $a\in\reals$ and $b\in\reals$ and $a < b$.
    Then there exists $p\in\rationals$ such that $p\in\intervalopen{a}{b}$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    \begin{byCase}
    \caseOf{$0 < b$.}
        \begin{byCase}
        \caseOf{$a < 0$.}
            There exists $r\in\rationals$ such that $r\in\intervalopen{a}{b}$ by \cref{real_integers,real_one_in_naturals,real_mul_ident,singleton_iff,setminus_intro,real_add_ident,real_mul_inverse,real_div,real_mul_zero,real_rationals,intervalopen}.
        \caseOf{$0 \leq a$.}
            There exists $r\in\rationals$ such that $r\in\intervalopen{a}{b}$ by \cref{real_rationals_nonneg_interval,real_integers,real_naturals_subset_reals,subseteq,real_mul_ident,real_zero_lt_one,real_one_leq_naturalsplus,real_lt_trans,real_pos_nonzero,singleton_iff,setminus_intro,real_rationals}.
        \end{byCase}
    \caseOf{$b \leq 0$.}
        Take $n, N$ such that $n\in\naturalsPlus$ and $N\in\naturalsPlus$ and $n / N\in\intervalopen{\neg{b}}{\neg{a}}$ by \cref{real_add_inverse,real_add_ident,real_add_inverse_unique,real_leq_neg,real_lt_neg,real_rationals_nonneg_interval}.
        Let $p = \neg{(n / N)}$.
        $n\in\reals$ by \cref{real_naturals_subset_reals,subseteq}.
        $\neg{n}\in\reals$ by \cref{real_add_inverse}.
        $\neg{\neg{n}} = n$ by \cref{real_naturals_subset_reals,subseteq,real_neg_neg}.
        $\neg{n}\in\integers$ by \cref{real_integers,real_naturals_subset_reals,subseteq,real_add_inverse}.
        $N\in\reals$ by \cref{real_naturals_subset_reals,subseteq}.
        $0 < N$ by \cref{real_add_ident,real_mul_ident,real_zero_lt_one,real_one_leq_naturalsplus,real_lt_trans}.
        $N\neq 0$ by \cref{real_pos_nonzero}.
        $\inv{N}\in\reals$ by \cref{real_mul_inverse}.
        $N\in\integers$ by \cref{real_integers}.
        $N\in\integers\setminus\{0\}$ by \cref{singleton_iff,setminus_intro}.
        $(\neg{n}) / N = p$ by \cref{real_div,real_mul_comm,real_mul_neg_right}.
        Thus $p\in\rationals$ by \cref{real_rationals}.
        Hence there exists $r\in\rationals$ such that $r\in\intervalopen{a}{b}$ by \cref{real_add_inverse,intervalopen,real_neg_neg,real_lt_neg}.
    \end{byCase}
\end{proof}

\subsubsection{Results involving absolute values}

% absolute value is nonnegative
\begin{proposition}\label{real_abs_nonnegative}
    Suppose $a\in\reals$.
    Then $\abs{a} \geq 0$.
\end{proposition}

% absolute value zero only at zero
\begin{proposition}\label{real_abs_eq_zero}
    Suppose $a\in\reals$ and $\abs{a} = 0$.
    Then $a = 0$.
\end{proposition}

% absolute value is symmetric
\begin{proposition}\label{real_abs_sub_swap}
    Suppose $a\in\reals$ and $b\in\reals$.
    Then $\abs{a - b} = \abs{b - a}$.
\end{proposition}

% helper lemma 12: absolute value is real
\begin{lemma}\label{real_abs_in_reals}
    Suppose $a\in\reals$.
    Then $\abs{a}\in\reals$.
\end{lemma}

% absolute value of a product
\begin{proposition}\label{real_abs_mul}
    Suppose $a\in\reals$ and $b\in\reals$.
    Then $\abs{a \rmul b} = \abs{a} \rmul \abs{b}$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    \begin{byCase}
    \caseOf{$a \geq 0$ and $b \geq 0$.}
        Follows by \cref{real_abs_nonneg,real_mul_closed,real_mul_zero,real_mul_comm,real_mul_pos_pos_gt}.
    \caseOf{$a \geq 0$ and $b < 0$.}
        Follows by \cref{real_abs_nonneg,real_abs_neg,real_mul_closed,real_mul_zero,real_mul_neg_opposite_sign_right,real_mul_neg_right,real_add_inverse}.
    \caseOf{$a < 0$ and $b \geq 0$.}
        Follows by \cref{real_abs_nonneg,real_abs_neg,real_mul_closed,real_mul_zero,real_mul_comm,real_mul_neg_opposite_sign_left,real_mul_neg_right,real_add_inverse}.
    \caseOf{$a < 0$ and $b < 0$.}
        Follows by \cref{real_abs_neg,real_abs_nonneg,real_mul_closed,real_mul_neg_neg_gt,real_mul_neg_right,real_mul_comm,real_neg_neg,real_add_inverse}.
    \end{byCase}
\end{proof}

% absolute value bounded by a two-sided bound
\begin{proposition}\label{real_abs_leq_if_pm_leq}
    Suppose $a\in\reals$ and $b\in\reals$.
    Suppose $a \leq b$ and $\neg{a} \leq b$.
    Then $\abs{a} \leq b$.
\end{proposition}

% absolute value squared
\begin{proposition}\label{real_abs_square}
    Suppose $a\in\reals$.
    Then $\exp{\abs{a}}{2} = \exp{a}{2}$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    Follows by \cref{def_of_2,real_abs_mul,real_abs_in_reals,real_abs_nonnegative,real_abs_nonneg,real_sq_nonnegative,real_one_in_naturals,real_pow_succ,real_pow_one,real_mul_closed}.
\end{proof}

% absolute value dominates
\begin{proposition}\label{real_leq_abs}
    Suppose $a\in\reals$.
    Then $a \leq \abs{a}$.
\end{proposition}

% negative is at most absolute value
\begin{proposition}\label{real_neg_leq_abs}
    Suppose $a\in\reals$.
    Then $\neg{a} \leq \abs{a}$.
\end{proposition}

% absolute value bound implies right bound
\begin{proposition}\label{real_abs_leq_imp_right_bound}
    Suppose $a\in\reals$ and $r\in\reals$ and $r$ is nonnegative.
    Suppose $\abs{a} \leq r$.
    Then $a \leq r$.
\end{proposition}

% absolute value bound implies left bound
\begin{proposition}\label{real_abs_leq_imp_left_bound}
    Suppose $a\in\reals$ and $r\in\reals$ and $r$ is nonnegative.
    Suppose $\abs{a} \leq r$.
    Then $\neg{r} \leq a$.
\end{proposition}

% two-sided bound implies absolute value bound
\begin{proposition}\label{real_abs_leq_from_bounds}
    Suppose $a\in\reals$ and $r\in\reals$ and $r$ is nonnegative.
    Suppose $\neg{r} \leq a$ and $a \leq r$.
    Then $\abs{a} \leq r$.
\end{proposition}

% absolute value bound implies left translated bound
\begin{proposition}\label{real_abs_sub_leq_imp_left_bound}
    Suppose $a\in\reals$ and $b\in\reals$ and $r\in\reals$ and $r$ is nonnegative.
    Suppose $\abs{a - b} \leq r$.
    Then $b - r \leq a$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    Follows by \cref{real_add_inverse,real_sub,real_add_closed,real_abs_leq_imp_left_bound,real_leq_add,real_add_comm,real_add_assoc,real_add_ident}.
\end{proof}

% absolute value bound implies right translated bound
\begin{proposition}\label{real_abs_sub_leq_imp_right_bound}
    Suppose $a\in\reals$ and $b\in\reals$ and $r\in\reals$ and $r$ is nonnegative.
    Suppose $\abs{a - b} \leq r$.
    Then $a \leq b + r$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    Follows by \cref{real_add_inverse,real_sub,real_add_closed,real_abs_leq_imp_right_bound,real_leq_add,real_add_assoc,real_add_comm,real_add_ident}.
\end{proof}

% Triangle Inequality
\begin{proposition}\label{real_triangle_inequality}
    Suppose $a\in\reals$ and $b\in\reals$.
    Then $\abs{a + b} \leq \abs{a} + \abs{b}$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    We have $\neg{(a + b)} \leq \abs{a} + \abs{b}$ by \cref{real_abs_in_reals,real_add_inverse,real_neg_leq_abs,real_leq_add,real_add_closed,real_add_comm,real_leq_trans,real_neg_addition}.
    Hence $\abs{a + b} \leq \abs{a} + \abs{b}$ by \cref{real_abs_in_reals,real_leq_abs,real_leq_add,real_add_closed,real_add_comm,real_leq_trans,real_abs_leq_if_pm_leq}.
\end{proof}

% Reverse Triangle Inequality
\begin{proposition}\label{real_reverse_triangle_inequality}
    Suppose $a\in\reals$ and $b\in\reals$.
    Then $\abs{a - b} \geq \abs{\abs{a} - \abs{b}}$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    $\abs{a}\in\reals$ by \cref{real_abs_in_reals}.
    Now $\abs{b}\in\reals$ by \cref{real_abs_in_reals}.
    $\abs{a - b}\in\reals$ by \cref{real_add_inverse,real_sub,real_add_closed,real_abs_in_reals}.
    $\abs{b - a} = \abs{a - b}$ by \cref{real_abs_sub_swap}.
    $\abs{(a - b) + b} \leq \abs{a - b} + \abs{b}$ by \cref{real_add_inverse,real_sub,real_add_closed,real_triangle_inequality}.
    $\abs{(b - a) + a} \leq \abs{b - a} + \abs{a}$ by \cref{real_add_inverse,real_sub,real_add_closed,real_triangle_inequality}.
    Thus $\abs{b} + \neg{\abs{a}} \leq \abs{b - a}$ by \cref{real_sub,real_add_assoc,real_add_inverse,real_add_comm,real_add_ident,real_add_closed,real_leq_add}.
    Therefore $\abs{\abs{a} - \abs{b}} \leq \abs{a - b}$ by \cref{real_sub,real_add_assoc,real_add_inverse,real_add_comm,real_add_ident,real_add_closed,real_leq_add,real_abs_sub_swap,real_sub_swap_neg,real_abs_leq_if_pm_leq}.
\end{proof}

\section{Standard Library: Cardinality}\label{form_sec_cardinality}

\begin{definition}\label{finite}
    $X$ is finite iff $X$ is empty or there exists $k \in \naturalsPlus$ such that
        there exists a bijection from $\initialSegment{k}$ to $X$.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma} \label{emptyset_is_finite}
    $\emptyset$ is finite.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    Follows by \cref{finite,emptyset}.
\end{proof}

% from §12 helper lemma 1: the first positive initial segment is a singleton
\begin{lemma}\label{initial_segment_one_library}
    $\initialSegment{1} = \{1\}$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    Follows by \cref{setext,initial_segment_naturalsplus,singleton_iff,real_one_in_naturals,real_one_leq_naturalsplus,real_naturals_subset_reals,subseteq,real_leq_antisym}.
\end{proof}

% from §12 helper lemma 2: a positive initial segment splits at its last point
\begin{lemma}\label{initial_segment_succ}
    Suppose $n\in\naturalsPlus$.
    Then $\initialSegment{n+1} = \cons{n+1}{\initialSegment{n}}$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    $n\in\reals$ by \cref{real_naturals_subset_reals,subseteq}.
    $1\in\reals$ by \cref{real_one_in_naturals,real_naturals_subset_reals,subseteq}.
    Thus $n < n+1$ by \cref{real_zero_lt_one,real_add_ident,real_add_comm,real_lt_add}.
    Hence $n+1\in\initialSegment{n+1}$ by \cref{real_naturals_closed_succ,initial_segment_naturalsplus}.
    Every element of $\initialSegment{n}$ is an element of $\initialSegment{n+1}$ by \cref{initial_segment_naturalsplus,real_naturals_subset_reals,subseteq,real_naturals_closed_succ,real_leq_trans}.
    Thus $\initialSegment{n}\subseteq\initialSegment{n+1}$ by \cref{subseteq}.
    Thus $\cons{n+1}{\initialSegment{n}}\subseteq\initialSegment{n+1}$ by \cref{cons_subseteq_intro}.
    Every element of $\initialSegment{n+1}$ is an element of $\cons{n+1}{\initialSegment{n}}$ by \cref{initial_segment_naturalsplus,real_succ_discrete,cons_iff}.
    Thus $\initialSegment{n+1}\subseteq\cons{n+1}{\initialSegment{n}}$ by \cref{subseteq}.
    Follows by \cref{subseteq_antisymmetric}.
\end{proof}

% from §12 helper lemma 3: adjoining fresh points extends a bijection
\begin{lemma}\label{bijection_cons}
    Suppose $f\in\bijections{A}{B}$.
    Suppose $a\notin A$ and $b\notin B$.
    Then $\cons{(a,b)}{f}\in\bijections{\cons{a}{A}}{\cons{b}{B}}$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    Let $g = \cons{(a,b)}{f}$.
    $f$ is a function by \cref{bijection_is_function}.
    $f$ is injective by \cref{bijections}.
    $\dom{f}=A$ by \cref{bijections_dom}.
    $\ran{f}=B$ by \cref{bijections,ran_of_surj}.
    Thus for all $e$ we have $(a,e)\notin f$ by \cref{dom_intro}.
    Thus for all $e$ we have $(e,b)\notin f$ by \cref{ran_intro}.
    $g$ is a relation by \cref{relation,cons_iff}.
    Show that $g$ is right-unique.
    \begin{subproof}
        It suffices to show that for all $c,d,e$ such that $(c,d),(c,e)\in g$ we have $d=e$.
        Fix $c$.
        Fix $d$.
        Fix $e$.
        Assume $(c,d),(c,e)\in g$.
        \begin{byCase}
        \caseOf{$(c,d)=(a,b)$ and $(c,e)=(a,b)$.}
            Then $d=e$ by \cref{pair_eq_iff}.
        \caseOf{$(c,d),(c,e)\in f$.}
            Then $d=e$ by \cref{function_rightunique}.
        \end{byCase}
    \end{subproof}
    Thus $g$ is a function.
    $\dom{g}=\cons{a}{A}$ by \cref{dom_cons}.
    $\ran{g}=\cons{b}{B}$ by \cref{ran_cons}.
    For all $x\in\dom{g}$ we have $g(x)\in\cons{b}{B}$ by \cref{function_apply_elim,ran_intro}.
    Thus $g$ is a function to $\cons{b}{B}$.
    Hence $g\in\funs{\cons{a}{A}}{\cons{b}{B}}$ by \cref{funs_intro}.
    Hence $g\in\surj{\cons{a}{A}}{\cons{b}{B}}$ by \cref{funs_surj_iff}.
    Show that $g$ is injective.
    \begin{subproof}
        It suffices to show that for all $c,d,e$ such that $(c,d),(e,d)\in g$ we have $c=e$.
        Fix $c$.
        Fix $d$.
        Fix $e$.
        Assume $(c,d),(e,d)\in g$.
        \begin{byCase}
        \caseOf{$(c,d)=(a,b)$ and $(e,d)=(a,b)$.}
            Then $c=e$ by \cref{pair_eq_iff}.
        \caseOf{$(c,d),(e,d)\in f$.}
            Then $c=e$ by \cref{injective}.
        \end{byCase}
    \end{subproof}
    Follows by \cref{bijections}.
\end{proof}

% from §12 helper lemma 4: adjoining one point preserves finiteness
\begin{lemma}\label{finite_cons}
    Suppose $X$ is finite.
    Then $\cons{x}{X}$ is finite.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    \begin{byCase}
    \caseOf{$x\in X$.}
        $\cons{x}{X}=X$ by \cref{cons_absorb}.
        Thus $\cons{x}{X}$ is finite.
    \caseOf{$x\notin X$ and $X=\emptyset$.}
        Let $f=\identity{\emptyset}$.
        $f\in\bijections{\emptyset}{\emptyset}$ by \cref{id_elem_bijections}.
        Let $g=\cons{(1,x)}{f}$.
        $g\in\bijections{\cons{1}{\emptyset}}{\cons{x}{\emptyset}}$ by \cref{emptyset,bijection_cons}.
        $\cons{1}{\emptyset}=\initialSegment{1}$ by \cref{initial_segment_one_library}.
        Thus $\cons{x}{X}$ is finite by \cref{real_one_in_naturals,finite}.
    \caseOf{$x\notin X$ and $X\neq\emptyset$.}
        $X$ is inhabited by \cref{nonempty_inhabited}.
        Take $n\in\naturalsPlus$ such that there exists $f\in\bijections{\initialSegment{n}}{X}$ by \cref{finite}.
        Take $f$ such that $f\in\bijections{\initialSegment{n}}{X}$.
        $n\in\reals$ by \cref{real_naturals_subset_reals,subseteq}.
        $1\in\reals$ by \cref{real_one_in_naturals,real_naturals_subset_reals,subseteq}.
        Thus $n < n+1$ by \cref{real_zero_lt_one,real_add_ident,real_add_comm,real_lt_add}.
        Hence $n+1\neq n$ by \cref{real_lt_irreflexive}.
        Hence $n+1\notin\initialSegment{n}$ by \cref{initial_segment_naturalsplus,real_naturals_closed_succ,real_naturals_subset_reals,subseteq,real_lt_asym}.
        Let $g=\cons{(n+1,x)}{f}$.
        $g\in\bijections{\cons{n+1}{\initialSegment{n}}}{\cons{x}{X}}$ by \cref{bijection_cons}.
        $\cons{n+1}{\initialSegment{n}}=\initialSegment{n+1}$ by \cref{initial_segment_succ}.
        Thus $\cons{x}{X}$ is finite by \cref{real_naturals_closed_succ,finite}.
    \end{byCase}
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma} \label{pair_is_finite}
    Assume that $x$ is a set and assume that $y$ is a set.
    Then $\{x,y\}$ is finite.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    $\emptyset$ is finite by \cref{emptyset_is_finite}.
    $\cons{y}{\emptyset}$ is finite by \cref{finite_cons}.
    Thus $\{x,y\}$ is finite by \cref{finite_cons}.
\end{proof}

% from §12 helper lemma 5: the successor is outside the preceding initial segment
\begin{lemma}\label{initial_segment_last_fresh}
    Suppose $n\in\naturalsPlus$.
    Then $n+1\notin\initialSegment{n}$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    $n\in\reals$ by \cref{real_naturals_subset_reals,subseteq}.
    $1\in\reals$ by \cref{real_one_in_naturals,real_naturals_subset_reals,subseteq}.
    Thus $n < n+1$ by \cref{real_zero_lt_one,real_add_ident,real_add_comm,real_lt_add}.
    Hence $n+1\neq n$ by \cref{real_lt_irreflexive}.
    Follows by \cref{initial_segment_naturalsplus,real_naturals_closed_succ,real_naturals_subset_reals,subseteq,real_lt_asym}.
\end{proof}

% from §12 helper lemma 6: removing the last value restricts a bijection
\begin{lemma}\label{bijection_remove_last}
    Suppose $n\in\naturalsPlus$.
    Suppose $f\in\bijections{\initialSegment{n+1}}{Y}$.
    Then $\restrl{f}{\initialSegment{n}}\in\bijections{\initialSegment{n}}{Y\setminus\{f(n+1)\}}$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    Let $g=\restrl{f}{\initialSegment{n}}$.
    $f$ is a function by \cref{bijection_is_function}.
    $f$ is injective by \cref{bijections}.
    $\dom{f}=\initialSegment{n+1}$ by \cref{bijections_dom}.
    $\ran{f}=Y$ by \cref{bijections,ran_of_surj}.
    $\initialSegment{n}\subseteq\initialSegment{n+1}$ by \cref{initial_segment_succ,subseteq_cons_self}.
    $g$ is a function by \cref{restrl_of_function_is_function}.
    $\dom{g}=\initialSegment{n}$ by \cref{dom_of_restrl_eq_inter,inter_absorb_supseteq_left}.

    Show that $\ran{g}\subseteq Y\setminus\{f(n+1)\}$.
    \begin{subproof}
        It suffices to show that every element of $\ran{g}$ is an element of $Y\setminus\{f(n+1)\}$.
        Fix $z\in\ran{g}$.
        Take $a$ such that $(a,z)\in g$ by \cref{ran_iff}.
        Then $(a,z)\in f$ and $a\in\initialSegment{n}$ by \cref{restrl_iff}.
        Thus $z\in Y$ by \cref{ran_intro}.
        Show that $z\neq f(n+1)$.
        \begin{subproof}
            Suppose not.
            $n+1\in\dom{f}$ by \cref{initial_segment_succ,cons_left}.
            Thus $(n+1,f(n+1))\in f$ by \cref{function_apply_elim}.
            Hence $a=n+1$ by \cref{injective}.
            Contradiction by \cref{initial_segment_last_fresh}.
        \end{subproof}
        Thus $z\in Y\setminus\{f(n+1)\}$ by \cref{setminus_intro,singleton_iff}.
    \end{subproof}

    Show that $Y\setminus\{f(n+1)\}\subseteq\ran{g}$.
    \begin{subproof}
        It suffices to show that every element of $Y\setminus\{f(n+1)\}$ is an element of $\ran{g}$.
        Fix $z\in Y\setminus\{f(n+1)\}$.
        Then $z\neq f(n+1)$ by \cref{setminus_elim_right,singleton_iff}.
        $z\in Y$ by \cref{setminus_elim_left}.
        Take $a\in\dom{f}$ such that $f(a)=z$ by \cref{fun_ran_iff}.
        $a=n+1$ or $a\in\initialSegment{n}$ by \cref{initial_segment_succ,cons_iff}.
        Thus $a\in\initialSegment{n}$.
        $(a,z)\in f$ by \cref{function_apply_elim}.
        Hence $(a,z)\in g$ by \cref{restrl_iff}.
        Thus $z\in\ran{g}$ by \cref{ran_intro}.
    \end{subproof}
    Hence $\ran{g}=Y\setminus\{f(n+1)\}$ by \cref{subseteq_antisymmetric}.
    For all $a\in\dom{g}$ we have $g(a)\in Y\setminus\{f(n+1)\}$ by \cref{function_apply_elim,ran_intro}.
    Thus $g$ is a function to $Y\setminus\{f(n+1)\}$.
    Hence $g\in\funs{\initialSegment{n}}{Y\setminus\{f(n+1)\}}$ by \cref{funs_intro}.
    Thus $g\in\surj{\initialSegment{n}}{Y\setminus\{f(n+1)\}}$ by \cref{funs_surj_iff}.
    $g$ is injective by \cref{restrl_injective}.
    Follows by \cref{bijections}.
\end{proof}

% from §12 helper lemma 7: induction over finite sets by adjoining points
\begin{lemma}\label{finite_induction}
    Suppose $X$ is finite.
    Suppose $\emptyset\in C$.
    Suppose for all $Y,y$ such that $Y\in C$ and $y\in X$ we have $\cons{y}{Y}\in C$.
    Then $X\in C$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    \begin{byCase}
    \caseOf{$X=\emptyset$.}
        Then $X\in C$.
    \caseOf{$X\neq\emptyset$.}
        Let $S=\{n\in\naturalsPlus\mid \text{for all $Y,f$ such that $Y\subseteq X$ and $f\in\bijections{\initialSegment{n}}{Y}$ we have $Y\in C$}\}$.
        $S\subseteq\naturalsPlus$ by \cref{subseteq}.

        Show that $1\in S$.
        \begin{subproof}
            $1\in\naturalsPlus$ by \cref{real_one_in_naturals}.
            Show that for all $Y,f$ such that $Y\subseteq X$ and $f\in\bijections{\initialSegment{1}}{Y}$ we have $Y\in C$.
            \begin{subproof}
                Fix $Y$.
                Fix $f$.
                Assume $Y\subseteq X$ and $f\in\bijections{\initialSegment{1}}{Y}$.
                $f$ is a function by \cref{bijection_is_function}.
                $\dom{f}=\initialSegment{1}$ by \cref{bijections_dom}.
                $\ran{f}=Y$ by \cref{bijections,ran_of_surj}.
                Let $y=f(1)$.
                $1\in\dom{f}$ by \cref{initial_segment_one_library,singleton_intro}.
                Thus $y\in Y$ by \cref{function_apply_elim,ran_intro}.
                Hence $y\in X$ by \cref{elem_subseteq}.
                For all $z\in Y$ we have $z=y$ by \cref{fun_ran_iff,initial_segment_one_library,singleton_iff}.
                Thus $Y$ is a subsingleton.
                Hence $Y=\{y\}$ by \cref{singleton_iff_inhabited_subsingleton}.
                $\cons{y}{\emptyset}\in C$ by assumption.
                Thus $Y\in C$ by assumption.
            \end{subproof}
            Follows by definition.
        \end{subproof}

        Show that for all $n\in S$ we have $n+1\in S$.
        \begin{subproof}
            Fix $n\in S$.
            Show that for all $Y,f$ such that $Y\subseteq X$ and $f\in\bijections{\initialSegment{n+1}}{Y}$ we have $Y\in C$.
            \begin{subproof}
                Fix $Y$.
                Fix $f$.
                Assume $Y\subseteq X$ and $f\in\bijections{\initialSegment{n+1}}{Y}$.
                $n\in\naturalsPlus$.
                $f$ is a function by \cref{bijection_is_function}.
                $\dom{f}=\initialSegment{n+1}$ by \cref{bijections_dom}.
                $\ran{f}=Y$ by \cref{bijections,ran_of_surj}.
                Let $y=f(n+1)$.
                $n+1\in\dom{f}$ by \cref{initial_segment_succ,cons_left}.
                Thus $y\in Y$ by \cref{function_apply_elim,ran_intro}.
                Hence $y\in X$ by \cref{elem_subseteq}.
                Let $g=\restrl{f}{\initialSegment{n}}$.
                $g\in\bijections{\initialSegment{n}}{Y\setminus\{y\}}$ by \cref{bijection_remove_last}.
                $Y\setminus\{y\}\subseteq X$ by \cref{setminus_subseteq,subseteq_transitive}.
                Thus $Y\setminus\{y\}\in C$ by definition.
                Hence $\cons{y}{Y\setminus\{y\}}\in C$ by assumption.
                $\cons{y}{Y\setminus\{y\}}=Y$ by \cref{cons_remove}.
                Thus $Y\in C$ by assumption.
            \end{subproof}
            Thus $n+1\in S$ by \cref{real_naturals_closed_succ}.
        \end{subproof}

        $1\in S$ and for all $n\in S$ we have $n+1\in S$.
        Thus $\naturalsPlus\subseteq S$ by \cref{real_naturals_induction,subseteq}.
        $X$ is inhabited by \cref{nonempty_inhabited}.
        Take $n\in\naturalsPlus$ such that there exists $f\in\bijections{\initialSegment{n}}{X}$ by \cref{finite}.
        Take $f$ such that $f\in\bijections{\initialSegment{n}}{X}$.
        $n\in S$ by \cref{elem_subseteq}.
        $X\subseteq X$ by \cref{subseteq_refl}.
        Thus $X\in C$ by definition.
    \end{byCase}
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma} \label{subseteq_finite_is_finite}
    Suppose $A\subseteq B$.
    Suppose $B$ is finite.
    Then $A$ is finite.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    Let $C=\{Y\in\pow{B}\mid \text{for all $D$ such that $D\subseteq Y$ we have $D$ is finite}\}$.
    Show that $\emptyset\in C$.
    \begin{subproof}
        $\emptyset\in\pow{B}$ by \cref{powerset_bottom}.
        Show that for all $D$ such that $D\subseteq\emptyset$ we have $D$ is finite.
        \begin{subproof}
            Fix $D$.
            Assume $D\subseteq\emptyset$.
            Then $D=\emptyset$ by \cref{subseteq_emptyset_iff}.
            Thus $D$ is finite by \cref{emptyset_is_finite}.
        \end{subproof}
        Follows by definition.
    \end{subproof}
    Show that for all $Y,y$ such that $Y\in C$ and $y\in B$ we have $\cons{y}{Y}\in C$.
    \begin{subproof}
        Fix $Y$.
        Fix $y$.
        Assume $Y\in C$ and $y\in B$.
        $Y\in\pow{B}$ by definition.
        Hence $Y\subseteq B$ by \cref{powerset_elim}.
        Thus $\cons{y}{Y}\subseteq B$ by \cref{cons_subseteq_intro}.
        Hence $\cons{y}{Y}\in\pow{B}$ by \cref{powerset_intro}.
        Show that for all $D$ such that $D\subseteq\cons{y}{Y}$ we have $D$ is finite.
        \begin{subproof}
            Fix $D$.
            Assume $D\subseteq\cons{y}{Y}$.
            $D\subseteq Y \lor (y\in D \land \remove{y}{D}\subseteq Y)$ by \cref{subseteq_cons_elim}.
            \begin{byCase}
            \caseOf{$D\subseteq Y$.}
                Then $D$ is finite by definition.
            \caseOf{$D\not\subseteq Y$.}
                $y\in D \land \remove{y}{D}\subseteq Y$ by assumption.
                Thus $\remove{y}{D}$ is finite by definition.
                Hence $\cons{y}{\remove{y}{D}}$ is finite by \cref{finite_cons}.
                $\cons{y}{\remove{y}{D}}=D$ by \cref{cons_remove}.
                Thus $D$ is finite by assumption.
            \end{byCase}
        \end{subproof}
        Follows by definition.
    \end{subproof}
    $B\in C$ by \cref{finite_induction}.
    Thus $A$ is finite by definition.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma} \label{union_of_finite_is_finite}
    Suppose $A$ is finite and $B$ is finite.
    Then $A\union B$ is finite.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    Let $C=\{Y\in\pow{A}\mid \text{$Y\union B$ is finite}\}$.
    Show that $\emptyset\in C$.
    \begin{subproof}
        $\emptyset\in\pow{A}$ by \cref{powerset_bottom}.
        $\emptyset\union B=B$ by \cref{union_comm,union_emptyset}.
        Thus $\emptyset\union B$ is finite by assumption.
        Follows by definition.
    \end{subproof}
    Show that for all $Y,y$ such that $Y\in C$ and $y\in A$ we have $\cons{y}{Y}\in C$.
    \begin{subproof}
        Fix $Y$.
        Fix $y$.
        Assume $Y\in C$ and $y\in A$.
        $Y\in\pow{A}$ by definition.
        Hence $Y\subseteq A$ by \cref{powerset_elim}.
        Thus $\cons{y}{Y}\subseteq A$ by \cref{cons_subseteq_intro}.
        Hence $\cons{y}{Y}\in\pow{A}$ by \cref{powerset_intro}.
        $Y\union B$ is finite by definition.
        Hence $\cons{y}{Y\union B}$ is finite by \cref{finite_cons}.
        $\cons{y}{Y}\union B=\cons{y}{Y\union B}$ by \cref{union_cons}.
        Thus $\cons{y}{Y}\union B$ is finite by assumption.
        Follows by definition.
    \end{subproof}
    $A\in C$ by \cref{finite_induction}.
    Thus $A\union B$ is finite by definition.
\end{proof}

\begin{abbreviation}\label{infinite}
    $X$ is infinite iff $X$ is not finite.
\end{abbreviation}

% \begin{definition}\label{cardinality}
%     $X$ has cardinality $k$ iff $k$ is a natural number and
%     there exists a bijection from $k$ to $X$.
% \end{definition}

% from §12 helper lemma 8: adjoining a real preserves existence of a maximum
\begin{lemma}\label{maximum_cons}
    Suppose $Y\subseteq\reals$.
    Suppose $m$ is a maximum of $Y$.
    Suppose $x\in\reals$.
    Then there exists $p\in\cons{x}{Y}$ such that $p$ is a maximum of $\cons{x}{Y}$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    $m\in Y$ by \cref{real_maximum}.
    Hence $m\in\reals$ by \cref{elem_subseteq}.
    \begin{byCase}
    \caseOf{$x\leq m$.}
        $m\in\cons{x}{Y}$ by \cref{cons_right}.
        Show that for all $a\in\cons{x}{Y}$ we have $a\leq m$.
        \begin{subproof}
            Fix $a\in\cons{x}{Y}$.
            $a=x$ or $a\in Y$ by \cref{cons_iff}.
            \begin{byCase}
            \caseOf{$a=x$.}
                Then $a\leq m$ by assumption.
            \caseOf{$a\neq x$.}
                Then $a\in Y$ by assumption.
                Thus $a\leq m$ by \cref{real_maximum}.
            \end{byCase}
        \end{subproof}
        Thus $m$ is a maximum of $\cons{x}{Y}$ by \cref{real_maximum}.
        Thus there exists $p\in\cons{x}{Y}$ such that $p$ is a maximum of $\cons{x}{Y}$.
    \caseOf{$x\not\leq m$.}
        $x\neq m$ by definition.
        $x < m$ or $m < x$ by \cref{real_lt_trichotomy}.
        It is wrong that $x < m$ by definition.
        Hence $m < x$ by assumption.
        Thus $m\leq x$ by definition.
        $x\in\cons{x}{Y}$ by \cref{cons_left}.
        Show that for all $a\in\cons{x}{Y}$ we have $a\leq x$.
        \begin{subproof}
            Fix $a\in\cons{x}{Y}$.
            $a=x$ or $a\in Y$ by \cref{cons_iff}.
            \begin{byCase}
            \caseOf{$a=x$.}
                Then $a\leq x$ by definition.
            \caseOf{$a\neq x$.}
                Then $a\in Y$ by assumption.
                Hence $a\leq m$ by \cref{real_maximum}.
                $a\in\reals$ by \cref{elem_subseteq}.
                Thus $a\leq x$ by \cref{real_leq_trans}.
            \end{byCase}
        \end{subproof}
        Thus $x$ is a maximum of $\cons{x}{Y}$ by \cref{real_maximum}.
        Thus there exists $p\in\cons{x}{Y}$ such that $p$ is a maximum of $\cons{x}{Y}$.
    \end{byCase}
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}\label{finite_real_maximum}
    Suppose $A\subseteq\reals$.
    Suppose $A$ is finite.
    Suppose $A\neq \emptyset$.
    Then there exists $m\in A$ such that $m$ is a maximum of $A$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    Let $C=\{Y\in\pow{A}\mid \text{$Y=\emptyset$ or there exists $m\in Y$ such that $m$ is a maximum of $Y$}\}$.
    Show that $\emptyset\in C$.
    \begin{subproof}
        $\emptyset\in\pow{A}$ by \cref{powerset_bottom}.
        Follows by definition.
    \end{subproof}
    Show that for all $Y,y$ such that $Y\in C$ and $y\in A$ we have $\cons{y}{Y}\in C$.
    \begin{subproof}
        Fix $Y$.
        Fix $y$.
        Assume $Y\in C$ and $y\in A$.
        $Y\in\pow{A}$ by definition.
        Hence $Y\subseteq A$ by \cref{powerset_elim}.
        Thus $\cons{y}{Y}\subseteq A$ by \cref{cons_subseteq_intro}.
        Hence $\cons{y}{Y}\in\pow{A}$ by \cref{powerset_intro}.
        $y\in\reals$ by \cref{elem_subseteq}.
        \begin{byCase}
        \caseOf{$Y=\emptyset$.}
            $\cons{y}{Y}=\{y\}$ by assumption.
            $y\in\cons{y}{Y}$ by \cref{cons_left}.
            Show that for all $z\in\cons{y}{Y}$ we have $z\leq y$.
            \begin{subproof}
                Fix $z\in\cons{y}{Y}$.
                Then $z=y$ by \cref{singleton_iff}.
                Thus $z\leq y$ by definition.
            \end{subproof}
            Thus $y$ is a maximum of $\cons{y}{Y}$ by \cref{real_maximum}.
            Hence there exists $p\in\cons{y}{Y}$ such that $p$ is a maximum of $\cons{y}{Y}$.
            Thus $\cons{y}{Y}\in C$ by definition.
        \caseOf{$Y\neq\emptyset$.}
            Take $m\in Y$ such that $m$ is a maximum of $Y$ by definition.
            $Y\subseteq\reals$ by \cref{subseteq_transitive}.
            Thus there exists $p\in\cons{y}{Y}$ such that $p$ is a maximum of $\cons{y}{Y}$ by \cref{maximum_cons}.
            Thus $\cons{y}{Y}\in C$ by definition.
        \end{byCase}
    \end{subproof}
    $A\in C$ by \cref{finite_induction}.
    Thus there exists $m\in A$ such that $m$ is a maximum of $A$ by definition.
\end{proof}

% from §12 helper lemma 9: adjoining a real preserves existence of a minimum
\begin{lemma}\label{minimum_cons}
    Suppose $Y\subseteq\reals$.
    Suppose $m$ is a minimum of $Y$.
    Suppose $x\in\reals$.
    Then there exists $p\in\cons{x}{Y}$ such that $p$ is a minimum of $\cons{x}{Y}$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    $m\in Y$ by \cref{real_minimum}.
    Hence $m\in\reals$ by \cref{elem_subseteq}.
    \begin{byCase}
    \caseOf{$m\leq x$.}
        $m\in\cons{x}{Y}$ by \cref{cons_right}.
        Show that for all $a\in\cons{x}{Y}$ we have $m\leq a$.
        \begin{subproof}
            Fix $a\in\cons{x}{Y}$.
            $a=x$ or $a\in Y$ by \cref{cons_iff}.
            \begin{byCase}
            \caseOf{$a=x$.}
                Then $m\leq a$ by assumption.
            \caseOf{$a\neq x$.}
                Then $a\in Y$ by assumption.
                Thus $m\leq a$ by \cref{real_minimum}.
            \end{byCase}
        \end{subproof}
        Thus $m$ is a minimum of $\cons{x}{Y}$ by \cref{real_minimum}.
        Thus there exists $p\in\cons{x}{Y}$ such that $p$ is a minimum of $\cons{x}{Y}$.
    \caseOf{$m\not\leq x$.}
        $m\neq x$ by definition.
        $m < x$ or $x < m$ by \cref{real_lt_trichotomy}.
        It is wrong that $m < x$ by definition.
        Hence $x < m$ by assumption.
        Thus $x\leq m$ by definition.
        $x\in\cons{x}{Y}$ by \cref{cons_left}.
        Show that for all $a\in\cons{x}{Y}$ we have $x\leq a$.
        \begin{subproof}
            Fix $a\in\cons{x}{Y}$.
            $a=x$ or $a\in Y$ by \cref{cons_iff}.
            \begin{byCase}
            \caseOf{$a=x$.}
                Then $x\leq a$ by definition.
            \caseOf{$a\neq x$.}
                Then $a\in Y$ by assumption.
                Hence $m\leq a$ by \cref{real_minimum}.
                $a\in\reals$ by \cref{elem_subseteq}.
                Thus $x\leq a$ by \cref{real_leq_trans}.
            \end{byCase}
        \end{subproof}
        Thus $x$ is a minimum of $\cons{x}{Y}$ by \cref{real_minimum}.
        Thus there exists $p\in\cons{x}{Y}$ such that $p$ is a minimum of $\cons{x}{Y}$.
    \end{byCase}
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}\label{finite_real_minimum}
    Suppose $A\subseteq\reals$.
    Suppose $A$ is finite.
    Suppose $A\neq \emptyset$.
    Then there exists $m\in A$ such that $m$ is a minimum of $A$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    Let $C=\{Y\in\pow{A}\mid \text{$Y=\emptyset$ or there exists $m\in Y$ such that $m$ is a minimum of $Y$}\}$.
    Show that $\emptyset\in C$.
    \begin{subproof}
        $\emptyset\in\pow{A}$ by \cref{powerset_bottom}.
        Follows by definition.
    \end{subproof}
    Show that for all $Y,y$ such that $Y\in C$ and $y\in A$ we have $\cons{y}{Y}\in C$.
    \begin{subproof}
        Fix $Y$.
        Fix $y$.
        Assume $Y\in C$ and $y\in A$.
        $Y\in\pow{A}$ by definition.
        Hence $Y\subseteq A$ by \cref{powerset_elim}.
        Thus $\cons{y}{Y}\subseteq A$ by \cref{cons_subseteq_intro}.
        Hence $\cons{y}{Y}\in\pow{A}$ by \cref{powerset_intro}.
        $y\in\reals$ by \cref{elem_subseteq}.
        \begin{byCase}
        \caseOf{$Y=\emptyset$.}
            $\cons{y}{Y}=\{y\}$ by assumption.
            $y\in\cons{y}{Y}$ by \cref{cons_left}.
            Show that for all $z\in\cons{y}{Y}$ we have $y\leq z$.
            \begin{subproof}
                Fix $z\in\cons{y}{Y}$.
                Then $z=y$ by \cref{singleton_iff}.
                Thus $y\leq z$ by definition.
            \end{subproof}
            Thus $y$ is a minimum of $\cons{y}{Y}$ by \cref{real_minimum}.
            Hence there exists $p\in\cons{y}{Y}$ such that $p$ is a minimum of $\cons{y}{Y}$.
            Thus $\cons{y}{Y}\in C$ by definition.
        \caseOf{$Y\neq\emptyset$.}
            Take $m\in Y$ such that $m$ is a minimum of $Y$ by definition.
            $Y\subseteq\reals$ by \cref{subseteq_transitive}.
            Thus there exists $p\in\cons{y}{Y}$ such that $p$ is a minimum of $\cons{y}{Y}$ by \cref{minimum_cons}.
            Thus $\cons{y}{Y}\in C$ by definition.
        \end{byCase}
    \end{subproof}
    $A\in C$ by \cref{finite_induction}.
    Thus there exists $m\in A$ such that $m$ is a minimum of $A$ by definition.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}\label{finite_naturals_maximum}
    Suppose $A\subseteq\naturalsPlus$.
    Suppose $A$ is finite.
    Suppose $A\neq \emptyset$.
    Then there exists $m\in A$ such that for all $a\in A$ we have $a \leq m$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    Follows by \cref{finite_real_maximum,real_maximum,real_naturals_subset_reals,subseteq_transitive}.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}\label{countable}
    $X$ is countable iff there exists $f\in\inj{X}{\naturals}$.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}\label{emptyset_is_countable}
    $\emptyset$ is countable.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    $\emptyset$ is a function to $\naturals$ by \cref{codom_of_emptyset_can_be_anything}.
    $\emptyset=\dom{\emptyset}$ by \cref{dom_emptyset}.
    Thus $\emptyset\in\funs{\emptyset}{\naturals}$ by \cref{funs_intro}.
    Thus $\emptyset\in\inj{\emptyset}{\naturals}$ by \cref{inj,emptyset}.
    Follows by \cref{countable}.
\end{proof}

% from §12 helper lemma 10: restricting the domain preserves an injection
\begin{lemma}\label{inj_restrl}
    Suppose $f\in\inj{B}{C}$.
    Suppose $A\subseteq B$.
    Then $\restrl{f}{A}\in\inj{A}{C}$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    $f\in\funs{B}{C}$ by \cref{inj}.
    Hence $f$ is a function by \cref{funs_is_function}.
    $\dom{f}=B$ by \cref{funs}.
    Let $g=\restrl{f}{A}$.
    $g$ is a function by \cref{restrl_of_function_is_function}.
    $\dom{g}=\dom{f}\inter A$ by \cref{dom_of_restrl_eq_inter}.
    Hence $\dom{g}=A$ by \cref{inter_absorb_supseteq_left}.
    Show that for all $x\in\dom{g}$ we have $g(x)\in C$.
    \begin{subproof}
        Fix $x\in\dom{g}$.
        Then $x\in A$ by \cref{restrl_dom}.
        Hence $x\in B$ by \cref{elem_subseteq}.
        $g(x)=f(x)$ by \cref{restrl_of_function_apply}.
        Thus $g(x)\in C$ by \cref{funs_type_apply}.
    \end{subproof}
    Thus $g$ is a function to $C$ by definition.
    $A=\dom{g}$ by assumption.
    Hence $g\in\funs{A}{C}$ by \cref{funs_intro}.
    $f$ is injective by \cref{inj,injective_function}.
    Thus $g$ is injective by \cref{restrl_injective}.
    Thus $g\in\inj{A}{C}$ by \cref{inj,injective_function}.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}\label{subseteq_countable_is_countable}
    Suppose $A\subseteq B$.
    Suppose $B$ is countable.
    Then $A$ is countable.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    Follows by \cref{countable,inj_restrl}.
\end{proof}

% from §12 helper lemma 11: natural numbers are real numbers
\begin{lemma}\label{naturals_subset_reals}
    $\naturals\subseteq\reals$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    Follows by \cref{naturals,union_iff,singleton_iff,real_naturals_subset_reals,subseteq,real_add_ident}.
\end{proof}

% from §12 helper lemma 12: doubling preserves natural numbers
\begin{lemma}\label{double_natural}
    Suppose $n\in\naturals$.
    Then $2\rmul n\in\naturals$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    Let $S=\{k\in\naturalsPlus\mid \text{$2\rmul k\in\naturalsPlus$}\}$.
    $S\subseteq\naturalsPlus$ by \cref{subseteq}.
    Show that $1\in S$.
    \begin{subproof}
        $2\in\naturalsPlus$ by \cref{def_of_2,real_one_in_naturals,real_naturals_closed_succ}.
        Hence $2\in\naturalsPlus$ by \cref{def_of_2}.
        $2\in\reals$ by \cref{real_naturals_subset_reals,elem_subseteq}.
        $2\rmul1=2$ by \cref{real_one_in_naturals,real_naturals_subset_reals,elem_subseteq,real_mul_comm,real_mul_ident}.
        $1\in\naturalsPlus$ by \cref{real_one_in_naturals}.
        Thus $1\in S$ by definition.
    \end{subproof}
    Show that for all $k\in S$ we have $k+1\in S$.
    \begin{subproof}
        Fix $k\in S$.
        $k\in\naturalsPlus$ and $2\rmul k\in\naturalsPlus$ by definition.
        Hence $(2\rmul k)+1\in\naturalsPlus$ by \cref{real_naturals_closed_succ}.
        Hence $((2\rmul k)+1)+1\in\naturalsPlus$ by \cref{real_naturals_closed_succ}.
        $k\in\reals$ by \cref{real_naturals_subset_reals,elem_subseteq}.
        $2\rmul(k+1)=((2\rmul k)+1)+1$ by \cref{def_of_2,real_one_in_naturals,real_naturals_subset_reals,elem_subseteq,real_add_closed,real_distrib,real_mul_ident,real_mul_comm,real_add_assoc}.
        Thus $2\rmul(k+1)\in\naturalsPlus$ by assumption.
        $k+1\in\naturalsPlus$ by \cref{real_naturals_closed_succ}.
        Thus $k+1\in S$ by definition.
    \end{subproof}
    $1\in S$ and for all $k\in S$ we have $k+1\in S$.
    Thus $\naturalsPlus\subseteq S$ by \cref{real_naturals_induction,subseteq}.
    \begin{byCase}
    \caseOf{$n=0$.}
        $2\in\reals$ by \cref{def_of_2,real_mul_ident,real_add_closed}.
        Then $2\rmul n=0$ by \cref{real_add_ident,real_mul_zero,real_mul_comm}.
        Thus $2\rmul n\in\naturals$ by \cref{naturals,union_iff,singleton_iff}.
    \caseOf{$n\neq0$.}
        Then $n\in\naturalsPlus$ by \cref{naturals,union_iff,singleton_iff}.
        Hence $n\in S$ by \cref{elem_subseteq}.
        Thus $2\rmul n\in\naturalsPlus$ by definition.
        Thus $2\rmul n\in\naturals$ by \cref{naturals,union_iff}.
    \end{byCase}
\end{proof}

% from §12 helper lemma 13: a successor of a doubled natural is natural
\begin{lemma}\label{double_succ_natural}
    Suppose $n\in\naturals$.
    Then $(2\rmul n)+1\in\naturals$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    Let $k=2\rmul n$.
    $k\in\naturals$ by \cref{double_natural}.
    \begin{byCase}
    \caseOf{$k=0$.}
        Then $k+1=1$ by \cref{real_add_ident,real_add_comm,real_mul_ident}.
        Thus $k+1\in\naturalsPlus$ by \cref{real_one_in_naturals}.
        Thus $k+1\in\naturals$ by \cref{naturals,union_iff}.
    \caseOf{$k\neq0$.}
        Then $k\in\naturalsPlus$ by \cref{naturals,union_iff,singleton_iff}.
        Hence $k+1\in\naturalsPlus$ by \cref{real_naturals_closed_succ}.
        Thus $k+1\in\naturals$ by \cref{naturals,union_iff}.
    \end{byCase}
\end{proof}

% from §12 helper lemma 14: doubling is injective on natural numbers
\begin{lemma}\label{double_injective}
    Suppose $m,n\in\naturals$.
    Suppose $2\rmul m=2\rmul n$.
    Then $m=n$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    $m,n\in\reals$ by \cref{naturals_subset_reals,elem_subseteq}.
    $1 < 2$ by \cref{def_of_2,real_zero_lt_one,real_add_ident,real_mul_ident,real_lt_add}.
    Hence $0 < 2$ by \cref{def_of_2,real_zero_lt_one,real_add_ident,real_mul_ident,real_add_closed,real_lt_trans}.
    $2\in\reals$ by \cref{def_of_2,real_mul_ident,real_add_closed}.
    Thus $2\neq0$ by \cref{real_pos_nonzero}.
    Follows by \cref{real_mul_cancel}.
\end{proof}

% from §12 helper lemma 15: a larger natural is at least the successor
\begin{lemma}\label{natural_succ_leq}
    Suppose $n,m\in\naturals$.
    Suppose $n < m$.
    Then $n+1\leq m$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    $n,m\in\reals$ by \cref{naturals_subset_reals,elem_subseteq}.
    \begin{byCase}
    \caseOf{$n=0$.}
        Then $n+1=1$ by \cref{real_add_ident,real_mul_ident}.
        $m\neq0$ by \cref{real_lt_irreflexive}.
        Hence $m\in\naturalsPlus$ by \cref{naturals,union_iff,singleton_iff}.
        $1 < m$ or $1=m$ by \cref{real_one_leq_naturalsplus}.
        Thus $n+1\leq m$ by definition.
    \caseOf{$n\neq0$.}
        Then $n\in\naturalsPlus$ by \cref{naturals,union_iff,singleton_iff}.
        $0 < n$ by \cref{real_one_leq_naturalsplus,real_zero_lt_one,real_naturals_subset_reals,elem_subseteq,real_add_ident,real_mul_ident,real_lt_trans}.
        Hence $0 < m$ by \cref{real_add_ident,real_lt_trans}.
        Thus $m\neq0$ by \cref{real_pos_nonzero}.
        Hence $m\in\naturalsPlus$ by \cref{naturals,union_iff,singleton_iff}.
        $n+1\in\naturalsPlus$ by \cref{real_naturals_closed_succ}.
        \begin{byCase}
        \caseOf{$m=n+1$.}
            Then $n+1\leq m$ by definition.
        \caseOf{$m\neq n+1$.}
            $n+1 < m$ or $m < n+1$ by \cref{real_lt_trichotomy,real_naturals_subset_reals,elem_subseteq}.
            It is wrong that $m < n+1$ by \cref{real_succ_discrete,real_lt_asym,real_lt_irreflexive}.
            Hence $n+1 < m$ by assumption.
            Thus $n+1\leq m$ by definition.
        \end{byCase}
    \end{byCase}
\end{proof}

% from §12 helper lemma 16: an odd code precedes the next even code
\begin{lemma}\label{double_succ_lt_next_double}
    Suppose $n\in\naturals$.
    Then $(2\rmul n)+1 < 2\rmul(n+1)$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    $n\in\reals$ by \cref{naturals_subset_reals,elem_subseteq}.
    $2\in\reals$ by \cref{def_of_2,real_mul_ident,real_add_closed}.
    Hence $2\rmul n\in\reals$ by \cref{real_mul_closed}.
    Thus $(2\rmul n)+1\in\reals$ by \cref{real_mul_ident,real_add_closed}.
    $0+((2\rmul n)+1) < 1+((2\rmul n)+1)$ by \cref{real_zero_lt_one,real_add_ident,real_mul_ident,real_lt_add}.
    Hence $(2\rmul n)+1 < ((2\rmul n)+1)+1$ by \cref{real_add_ident,real_mul_ident,real_add_comm}.
    $2\rmul(n+1)=((2\rmul n)+1)+1$ by \cref{def_of_2,real_mul_ident,real_add_closed,real_distrib,real_mul_comm,real_add_assoc}.
    Thus $(2\rmul n)+1 < 2\rmul(n+1)$ by assumption.
\end{proof}

% from §12 helper lemma 17: an even code precedes its odd successor
\begin{lemma}\label{double_lt_double_succ}
    Suppose $n\in\naturals$.
    Then $2\rmul n < (2\rmul n)+1$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    $n\in\reals$ by \cref{naturals_subset_reals,elem_subseteq}.
    $2\in\reals$ by \cref{def_of_2,real_mul_ident,real_add_closed}.
    Hence $2\rmul n\in\reals$ by \cref{real_mul_closed}.
    $0+(2\rmul n) < 1+(2\rmul n)$ by \cref{real_zero_lt_one,real_add_ident,real_mul_ident,real_lt_add}.
    Thus $2\rmul n < (2\rmul n)+1$ by \cref{real_add_ident,real_mul_ident,real_add_comm}.
\end{proof}

% from §12 helper lemma 18: even and odd natural codes are disjoint
\begin{lemma}\label{double_neq_double_succ}
    Suppose $m,n\in\naturals$.
    Then $2\rmul m\neq(2\rmul n)+1$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    $m,n\in\reals$ by \cref{naturals_subset_reals,elem_subseteq}.
    $1,2\in\reals$ by \cref{def_of_2,real_mul_ident,real_add_closed}.
    $0 < 2$ by \cref{def_of_2,real_zero_lt_one,real_add_ident,real_mul_ident,real_add_closed,real_lt_add,real_lt_trans}.
    Suppose not.
    \begin{byCase}
    \caseOf{$m=n$.}
        $2\rmul n < (2\rmul n)+1$ by \cref{double_lt_double_succ}.
        Contradiction by \cref{real_mul_closed,real_add_closed,real_lt_irreflexive}.
    \caseOf{$m\neq n$.}
        $m < n$ or $n < m$ by \cref{real_lt_trichotomy}.
        \begin{byCase}
        \caseOf{$m < n$.}
            $m\rmul2 < n\rmul2$ by \cref{real_lt_mul_pos}.
            Hence $2\rmul m < 2\rmul n$ by \cref{real_mul_comm}.
            $2\rmul n < (2\rmul n)+1$ by \cref{double_lt_double_succ}.
            Thus $2\rmul m < (2\rmul n)+1$ by \cref{real_mul_closed,real_add_closed,real_lt_trans}.
            Contradiction by \cref{real_mul_closed,real_add_closed,real_lt_irreflexive}.
        \caseOf{$m\not < n$.}
            Then $n < m$ by assumption.
            Hence $n+1\leq m$ by \cref{natural_succ_leq}.
            Show that $(2\rmul n)+1 < 2\rmul m$.
            \begin{subproof}
                \begin{byCase}
                \caseOf{$n+1=m$.}
                    Follows by \cref{double_succ_lt_next_double}.
                \caseOf{$n+1\neq m$.}
                    Then $n+1 < m$ by definition.
                    Hence $(n+1)\rmul2 < m\rmul2$ by \cref{real_lt_mul_pos,real_add_closed}.
                    Thus $2\rmul(n+1) < 2\rmul m$ by \cref{real_mul_comm,real_add_closed}.
                    $(2\rmul n)+1 < 2\rmul(n+1)$ by \cref{double_succ_lt_next_double}.
                    Follows by \cref{real_mul_closed,real_add_closed,real_lt_trans}.
                \end{byCase}
            \end{subproof}
            Contradiction by \cref{real_mul_closed,real_add_closed,real_lt_irreflexive}.
        \end{byCase}
    \end{byCase}
\end{proof}

% from §12 helper lemma 19: the even coding map is an injection
\begin{lemma}\label{even_code_injection}
    There exists $e\in\inj{\naturals}{\naturals}$ such that
    for all $n\in\naturals$ we have $e(n)=2\rmul n$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    Let $e=\{(n,2\rmul n)\mid n\in\naturals\}$.
    Show that $e$ is a relation.
    \begin{subproof}
        Show that for all $w\in e$ there exist $x,y$ such that $w=(x,y)$.
        \begin{subproof}
            Fix $w\in e$.
            Take $n\in\naturals$ such that $w=(n,2\rmul n)$.
            Thus there exist $x,y$ such that $w=(x,y)$.
        \end{subproof}
        Follows by \cref{relation}.
    \end{subproof}
    Show that $e$ is right-unique.
    \begin{subproof}
        Show that for all $a,b,b'$ such that $(a,b)\in e$ and $(a,b')\in e$ we have $b=b'$.
        \begin{subproof}
            Fix $a$.
            Fix $b$.
            Fix $b'$.
            Assume $(a,b)\in e$ and $(a,b')\in e$.
            Take $n\in\naturals$ such that $(a,b)=(n,2\rmul n)$.
            Take $k\in\naturals$ such that $(a,b')=(k,2\rmul k)$.
            $a=n$ and $b=2\rmul n$ by \cref{pair_eq_iff}.
            $a=k$ and $b'=2\rmul k$ by \cref{pair_eq_iff}.
            Thus $b=b'$.
        \end{subproof}
        Follows by \cref{rightunique}.
    \end{subproof}
    Thus $e$ is a function by definition.
    Show that $\naturals\subseteq\dom{e}$.
    \begin{subproof}
        Show that for all $n\in\naturals$ we have $n\in\dom{e}$.
        \begin{subproof}
            Fix $n\in\naturals$.
            $(n,2\rmul n)\in e$ by definition.
            Thus $n\in\dom{e}$ by \cref{dom_intro}.
        \end{subproof}
        Follows by \cref{subseteq}.
    \end{subproof}
    Show that $\dom{e}\subseteq\naturals$.
    \begin{subproof}
        Show that for all $x\in\dom{e}$ we have $x\in\naturals$.
        \begin{subproof}
            Fix $x\in\dom{e}$.
            Take $y$ such that $(x,y)\in e$ by \cref{dom_iff}.
            Take $n\in\naturals$ such that $(x,y)=(n,2\rmul n)$.
            Then $x=n$ by \cref{pair_eq_iff}.
            Thus $x\in\naturals$.
        \end{subproof}
        Follows by \cref{subseteq}.
    \end{subproof}
    Thus $\dom{e}=\naturals$ by \cref{subseteq_antisymmetric}.
    Show that for all $n\in\naturals$ we have $e(n)=2\rmul n$.
    \begin{subproof}
        Fix $n\in\naturals$.
        $(n,2\rmul n)\in e$ by definition.
        Thus $e(n)=2\rmul n$ by \cref{function_apply_intro}.
    \end{subproof}
    Show that for all $n\in\dom{e}$ we have $e(n)\in\naturals$.
    \begin{subproof}
        Fix $n\in\dom{e}$.
        Then $n\in\naturals$ by assumption.
        Hence $e(n)=2\rmul n$ by assumption.
        Thus $e(n)\in\naturals$ by \cref{double_natural}.
    \end{subproof}
    Thus $e$ is a function to $\naturals$ by definition.
    $\naturals=\dom{e}$ by assumption.
    Hence $e\in\funs{\naturals}{\naturals}$ by \cref{funs_intro}.
    Show that for all $m,n$ such that $m\in\naturals$ and $n\in\naturals$ and $e(m)=e(n)$ we have $m=n$.
    \begin{subproof}
        Fix $m$.
        Fix $n$.
        Assume $m\in\naturals$ and $n\in\naturals$ and $e(m)=e(n)$.
        $e(m)=2\rmul m$ and $e(n)=2\rmul n$ by assumption.
        Thus $2\rmul m=2\rmul n$.
        Follows by \cref{double_injective}.
    \end{subproof}
    Thus $e\in\inj{\naturals}{\naturals}$ by \cref{inj}.
    Hence there exists $q\in\inj{\naturals}{\naturals}$ such that
    for all $n\in\naturals$ we have $q(n)=2\rmul n$.
\end{proof}

% from §12 helper lemma 20: the odd coding map is an injection
\begin{lemma}\label{odd_code_injection}
    There exists $o\in\inj{\naturals}{\naturals}$ such that
    for all $n\in\naturals$ we have $o(n)=(2\rmul n)+1$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    Let $o=\{(n,(2\rmul n)+1)\mid n\in\naturals\}$.
    Show that $o$ is a relation.
    \begin{subproof}
        Show that for all $w\in o$ there exist $x,y$ such that $w=(x,y)$.
        \begin{subproof}
            Fix $w\in o$.
            Take $n\in\naturals$ such that $w=(n,(2\rmul n)+1)$.
            Thus there exist $x,y$ such that $w=(x,y)$.
        \end{subproof}
        Follows by \cref{relation}.
    \end{subproof}
    Show that $o$ is right-unique.
    \begin{subproof}
        Show that for all $a,b,b'$ such that $(a,b)\in o$ and $(a,b')\in o$ we have $b=b'$.
        \begin{subproof}
            Fix $a$.
            Fix $b$.
            Fix $b'$.
            Assume $(a,b)\in o$ and $(a,b')\in o$.
            Take $n\in\naturals$ such that $(a,b)=(n,(2\rmul n)+1)$.
            Take $k\in\naturals$ such that $(a,b')=(k,(2\rmul k)+1)$.
            $a=n$ and $b=(2\rmul n)+1$ by \cref{pair_eq_iff}.
            $a=k$ and $b'=(2\rmul k)+1$ by \cref{pair_eq_iff}.
            Thus $b=b'$.
        \end{subproof}
        Follows by \cref{rightunique}.
    \end{subproof}
    Thus $o$ is a function by definition.
    Show that $\naturals\subseteq\dom{o}$.
    \begin{subproof}
        Show that for all $n\in\naturals$ we have $n\in\dom{o}$.
        \begin{subproof}
            Fix $n\in\naturals$.
            $(n,(2\rmul n)+1)\in o$ by definition.
            Thus $n\in\dom{o}$ by \cref{dom_intro}.
        \end{subproof}
        Follows by \cref{subseteq}.
    \end{subproof}
    Show that $\dom{o}\subseteq\naturals$.
    \begin{subproof}
        Show that for all $x\in\dom{o}$ we have $x\in\naturals$.
        \begin{subproof}
            Fix $x\in\dom{o}$.
            Take $y$ such that $(x,y)\in o$ by \cref{dom_iff}.
            Take $n\in\naturals$ such that $(x,y)=(n,(2\rmul n)+1)$.
            Then $x=n$ by \cref{pair_eq_iff}.
            Thus $x\in\naturals$.
        \end{subproof}
        Follows by \cref{subseteq}.
    \end{subproof}
    Thus $\dom{o}=\naturals$ by \cref{subseteq_antisymmetric}.
    Show that for all $n\in\naturals$ we have $o(n)=(2\rmul n)+1$.
    \begin{subproof}
        Fix $n\in\naturals$.
        $(n,(2\rmul n)+1)\in o$ by definition.
        Thus $o(n)=(2\rmul n)+1$ by \cref{function_apply_intro}.
    \end{subproof}
    Show that for all $n\in\dom{o}$ we have $o(n)\in\naturals$.
    \begin{subproof}
        Fix $n\in\dom{o}$.
        Then $n\in\naturals$ by assumption.
        Hence $o(n)=(2\rmul n)+1$ by assumption.
        Thus $o(n)\in\naturals$ by \cref{double_succ_natural}.
    \end{subproof}
    Thus $o$ is a function to $\naturals$ by definition.
    $\naturals=\dom{o}$ by assumption.
    Hence $o\in\funs{\naturals}{\naturals}$ by \cref{funs_intro}.
    Show that for all $m,n$ such that $m\in\naturals$ and $n\in\naturals$ and $o(m)=o(n)$ we have $m=n$.
    \begin{subproof}
        Fix $m$.
        Fix $n$.
        Assume $m\in\naturals$ and $n\in\naturals$ and $o(m)=o(n)$.
        $o(m)=(2\rmul m)+1$ and $o(n)=(2\rmul n)+1$ by assumption.
        Thus $(2\rmul m)+1=(2\rmul n)+1$.
        $m,n\in\reals$ by \cref{naturals_subset_reals,elem_subseteq}.
        $1,2\in\reals$ by \cref{def_of_2,real_mul_ident,real_add_closed}.
        Hence $2\rmul m=2\rmul n$ by \cref{real_mul_closed,real_add_cancel}.
        Follows by \cref{double_injective}.
    \end{subproof}
    Thus $o\in\inj{\naturals}{\naturals}$ by \cref{inj}.
    Hence there exists $q\in\inj{\naturals}{\naturals}$ such that
    for all $n\in\naturals$ we have $q(n)=(2\rmul n)+1$.
\end{proof}

% from §12 helper lemma 21: disjoint injections combine into an injection
\begin{lemma}\label{union_inj_disjoint}
    Suppose $f\in\inj{A}{C}$ and $g\in\inj{B}{C}$.
    Suppose $A\inter B=\emptyset$ and $\ran{f}\inter\ran{g}=\emptyset$.
    Then $f\union g\in\inj{A\union B}{C}$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    $f\in\funs{A}{C}$ and $g\in\funs{B}{C}$ by \cref{inj}.
    Thus $f$ and $g$ are functions by \cref{funs_is_function}.
    $\dom{f}=A$ and $\dom{g}=B$ by \cref{funs}.
    $f$ and $g$ are injective by \cref{inj,injective_function}.
    Let $h=f\union g$.
    $h$ is a relation by \cref{union_relations_is_relation}.
    Show that $h$ is right-unique.
    \begin{subproof}
        Show that for all $x,y,z$ such that $(x,y)\in h$ and $(x,z)\in h$ we have $y=z$.
        \begin{subproof}
            Fix $x$.
            Fix $y$.
            Fix $z$.
            Assume $(x,y)\in h$ and $(x,z)\in h$.
            $(x,y)\in f$ or $(x,y)\in g$ by \cref{union_iff}.
            $(x,z)\in f$ or $(x,z)\in g$ by \cref{union_iff}.
            \begin{byCase}
            \caseOf{$(x,y)\in f$ and $(x,z)\in f$.}
                Thus $y=z$ by \cref{rightunique}.
            \caseOf{$(x,y)\in f$ and $(x,z)\in g$.}
                Show that $y=z$.
                \begin{subproof}
                    Suppose not.
                    $x\in\dom{f}$ and $x\in\dom{g}$ by \cref{dom_intro}.
                    Hence $x\in A\inter B$ by \cref{inter_intro}.
                    Thus $x\in\emptyset$ by assumption.
                    Contradiction by \cref{emptyset}.
                \end{subproof}
            \caseOf{$(x,y)\in g$ and $(x,z)\in f$.}
                Show that $y=z$.
                \begin{subproof}
                    Suppose not.
                    $x\in\dom{f}$ and $x\in\dom{g}$ by \cref{dom_intro}.
                    Hence $x\in A\inter B$ by \cref{inter_intro}.
                    Thus $x\in\emptyset$ by assumption.
                    Contradiction by \cref{emptyset}.
                \end{subproof}
            \caseOf{$(x,y)\in g$ and $(x,z)\in g$.}
                Thus $y=z$ by \cref{rightunique}.
            \end{byCase}
        \end{subproof}
        Follows by \cref{rightunique}.
    \end{subproof}
    Thus $h$ is a function by definition.
    $\dom{h}=\dom{f}\union\dom{g}$ by \cref{dom_union}.
    Hence $\dom{h}=A\union B$ by assumption.
    $\ran{f}\subseteq C$ and $\ran{g}\subseteq C$ by \cref{funs_ran}.
    Show that $\ran{h}\subseteq C$.
    \begin{subproof}
        Show that for all $c\in\ran{h}$ we have $c\in C$.
        \begin{subproof}
            Fix $c\in\ran{h}$.
            Then $c\in\ran{f}\union\ran{g}$ by \cref{ran_union}.
            Thus $c\in\ran{f}$ or $c\in\ran{g}$ by \cref{union_iff}.
            Hence $c\in C$ by \cref{elem_subseteq}.
        \end{subproof}
        Follows by \cref{subseteq}.
    \end{subproof}
    Show that for all $x\in\dom{h}$ we have $h(x)\in C$.
    \begin{subproof}
        Fix $x\in\dom{h}$.
        $(x,h(x))\in h$ by \cref{function_apply_elim}.
        Hence $h(x)\in\ran{h}$ by \cref{ran_intro}.
        Thus $h(x)\in C$ by \cref{elem_subseteq}.
    \end{subproof}
    Thus $h$ is a function to $C$ by definition.
    $A\union B=\dom{h}$ by assumption.
    Hence $h\in\funs{A\union B}{C}$ by \cref{funs_intro}.
    Show that $h$ is injective.
    \begin{subproof}
        Show that for all $x,x',y$ such that $(x,y)\in h$ and $(x',y)\in h$ we have $x=x'$.
        \begin{subproof}
            Fix $x$.
            Fix $x'$.
            Fix $y$.
            Assume $(x,y)\in h$ and $(x',y)\in h$.
            $(x,y)\in f$ or $(x,y)\in g$ by \cref{union_iff}.
            $(x',y)\in f$ or $(x',y)\in g$ by \cref{union_iff}.
            \begin{byCase}
            \caseOf{$(x,y)\in f$ and $(x',y)\in f$.}
                Thus $x=x'$ by \cref{injective}.
            \caseOf{$(x,y)\in f$ and $(x',y)\in g$.}
                Show that $x=x'$.
                \begin{subproof}
                    Suppose not.
                    $y\in\ran{f}$ and $y\in\ran{g}$ by \cref{ran_intro}.
                    Hence $y\in\ran{f}\inter\ran{g}$ by \cref{inter_intro}.
                    Thus $y\in\emptyset$ by assumption.
                    Contradiction by \cref{emptyset}.
                \end{subproof}
            \caseOf{$(x,y)\in g$ and $(x',y)\in f$.}
                Show that $x=x'$.
                \begin{subproof}
                    Suppose not.
                    $y\in\ran{f}$ and $y\in\ran{g}$ by \cref{ran_intro}.
                    Hence $y\in\ran{f}\inter\ran{g}$ by \cref{inter_intro}.
                    Thus $y\in\emptyset$ by assumption.
                    Contradiction by \cref{emptyset}.
                \end{subproof}
            \caseOf{$(x,y)\in g$ and $(x',y)\in g$.}
                Thus $x=x'$ by \cref{injective}.
            \end{byCase}
        \end{subproof}
        Follows by \cref{injective}.
    \end{subproof}
    Thus $h\in\inj{A\union B}{C}$ by \cref{inj,injective_function}.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}\label{union_of_countable_is_countable}
    Suppose $A$ is countable and $B$ is countable.
    Then $A\union B$ is countable.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    Take $f$ such that $f\in\inj{A}{\naturals}$ by \cref{countable}.
    Take $g$ such that $g\in\inj{B}{\naturals}$ by \cref{countable}.
    Take $e\in\inj{\naturals}{\naturals}$ such that
    for all $n\in\naturals$ we have $e(n)=2\rmul n$ by \cref{even_code_injection}.
    Take $o\in\inj{\naturals}{\naturals}$ such that
    for all $n\in\naturals$ we have $o(n)=(2\rmul n)+1$ by \cref{odd_code_injection}.
    Let $r=\restrl{g}{B\setminus A}$.
    $B\setminus A\subseteq B$ by \cref{setminus_subseteq}.
    Thus $r\in\inj{B\setminus A}{\naturals}$ by \cref{inj_restrl}.
    Let $F=e\circ f$.
    Let $G=o\circ r$.
    $F\in\inj{A}{\naturals}$ by \cref{inj_circ}.
    $G\in\inj{B\setminus A}{\naturals}$ by \cref{inj_circ}.
    $f\in\funs{A}{\naturals}$ and $e\in\funs{\naturals}{\naturals}$ by \cref{inj}.
    $r\in\funs{B\setminus A}{\naturals}$ and $o\in\funs{\naturals}{\naturals}$ by \cref{inj}.
    Thus $f$ and $e$ are functions by \cref{funs_is_function}.
    $r$ and $o$ are functions by \cref{funs_is_function}.
    $\ran{f}\subseteq\naturals$ and $\dom{e}=\naturals$ by \cref{funs_ran,funs}.
    Hence $e$ is composable with $f$ by definition.
    $\ran{r}\subseteq\naturals$ and $\dom{o}=\naturals$ by \cref{funs_ran,funs}.
    Hence $o$ is composable with $r$ by definition.
    $F$ and $G$ are functions by \cref{inj,funs_is_function}.
    Show that $\ran{F}\inter\ran{G}=\emptyset$.
    \begin{subproof}
        Show that for all $c$ we have $c\notin\ran{F}\inter\ran{G}$.
        \begin{subproof}
            Fix $c$.
            Show that $c\notin\ran{F}\inter\ran{G}$.
            \begin{subproof}
                Suppose not.
                $c\in\ran{F}$ and $c\in\ran{G}$ by \cref{inter_elim_left,inter_elim_right}.
                Take $a\in\dom{F}$ such that $c=F(a)$ by \cref{fun_ran_iff}.
                Take $b\in\dom{G}$ such that $c=G(b)$ by \cref{fun_ran_iff}.
                $F\in\funs{A}{\naturals}$ and $G\in\funs{B\setminus A}{\naturals}$ by \cref{inj}.
                Thus $\dom{F}=A$ and $\dom{G}=B\setminus A$ by \cref{funs}.
                Hence $a\in A$ and $b\in B\setminus A$ by assumption.
                Thus $f(a)\in\naturals$ and $r(b)\in\naturals$ by \cref{funs_type_apply}.
                $F(a)=e(f(a))$ and $G(b)=o(r(b))$ by \cref{circ_apply,funs}.
                $e(f(a))=2\rmul f(a)$ and $o(r(b))=(2\rmul r(b))+1$ by assumption.
                Hence $c=2\rmul f(a)$ and $c=(2\rmul r(b))+1$ by assumption.
                Thus $2\rmul f(a)=(2\rmul r(b))+1$.
                Contradiction by \cref{double_neq_double_succ}.
            \end{subproof}
        \end{subproof}
        Follows by \cref{setext,emptyset}.
    \end{subproof}
    $A\inter(B\setminus A)=\emptyset$ by \cref{setminus_disjoint}.
    Thus $F\union G\in\inj{A\union(B\setminus A)}{\naturals}$ by \cref{union_inj_disjoint}.
    $A\union(B\setminus A)=A\union B$ by \cref{setext,union_iff,setminus}.
    Hence $F\union G\in\inj{A\union B}{\naturals}$ by assumption.
    Follows by \cref{countable}.
\end{proof}

\section{Standard Library: Axiom of Choice and Consequences}

\begin{axiom}\label{axiom_of_choice}
    Suppose $R$ is a relation.
    Suppose for all $x\in A$ there exists $y$ such that $x\mathrel{R} y$.
    Then there exists $f$ such that $f$ is a function on $A$ and for all $x\in A$ we have $x\mathrel{R} f(x)$.
\end{axiom}

\begin{definition}\label{finite_intersection_property}
    $C$ has the finite intersection property iff
        $C$ is inhabited and
        for all $F$ such that $F\subseteq C$ and $F$ is finite and inhabited
        we have $\inters{F}\neq\emptyset$.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}\label{maximal_finite_intersection_property_on}
    $D$ is maximal with respect to the finite intersection property on $X$ iff
        $D\subseteq\pow{X}$ and
        $D$ has the finite intersection property and
        for all $E$ such that $D\subseteq E\subseteq\pow{X}$ and $E$ has the finite intersection property
            we have $E = D$.
\end{definition}

% from §13 helper definition 1: chains in a strict relation
\begin{definition}\label{chain_in_relation}
    $C$ is a chain in $P$ with respect to $Q$ iff
        $C\subseteq P$ and
        for all $x,y\in C$ we have $x = y$ or $x\mathrel{Q}y$ or $y\mathrel{Q}x$.
\end{definition}

% from §13 helper definition 2: maximal chains
\begin{definition}\label{maximal_chain_in_relation}
    $C$ is a maximal chain in $P$ with respect to $Q$ iff
        $C$ is a chain in $P$ with respect to $Q$ and
        for all $D$ such that $C\subseteq D$ and $D$ is a chain in $P$ with respect to $Q$
            we have $D = C$.
\end{definition}

% from §13 helper definition 3: strict well orderings used in the choice construction
\begin{definition}\label{foundational_ordering}
    $R$ is a foundational ordering on $B$ iff
        $R$ is a binary relation on $B$ and
        $R$ is transitive and
        $R$ is asymmetric and
        $R$ is connex on $B$ and
        for all $C$ such that $C\subseteq B$ and $C\neq\emptyset$
            there exists $c\in C$ such that for all $x\in C$ we have $c\mathrel{R}x$ or $c = x$.
\end{definition}

% from §13 helper definition 4: well orderings generated by a function
\begin{definition}\label{generated_well_ordering}
    $R$ is a well ordering generated by $f$ on $B$ iff
        $R$ is a foundational ordering on $B$ and
        for all $b\in B$ we have $f(\downward{R}{b}) = b$.
\end{definition}

% from §13 helper definition 5: common initial segments of generated orderings
\begin{definition}\label{common_initial_segment}
    $I$ is a common initial segment of $R$ on $B$ and $S$ on $C$ iff
        $I\subseteq B\inter C$ and
        for all $i\in I$ we have
            $\downward{R}{i} = \downward{S}{i}$ and
            $\downward{R}{i}\subseteq I$ and
            $\downward{S}{i}\subseteq I$.
\end{definition}

% from §13 helper lemma 1: the empty common initial segment
\begin{lemma}\label{empty_common_initial_segment}
    $\emptyset$ is a common initial segment of $R$ on $B$ and $S$ on $C$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    Follows by \cref{common_initial_segment,emptyset_subseteq,emptyset}.
\end{proof}

% from §13 helper lemma 2: unions of common initial segments
\begin{lemma}\label{union_common_initial_segments}
    Suppose every element of $K$ is a common initial segment of $R$ on $B$ and $S$ on $C$.
    Then $\unions{K}$ is a common initial segment of $R$ on $B$ and $S$ on $C$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    For all $J\in K$ we have $J\subseteq B\inter C$ by \cref{common_initial_segment}.
    Thus $\unions{K}\subseteq B\inter C$ by \cref{unions_family}.

    Show that for all $i\in\unions{K}$ we have $\downward{R}{i}=\downward{S}{i}$.
    \begin{subproof}
        Fix $i\in\unions{K}$.
        Take $J\in K$ such that $i\in J$ by \cref{unions_iff}.
        Follows by \cref{common_initial_segment}.
    \end{subproof}

    Show that for all $i\in\unions{K}$ we have $\downward{R}{i}\subseteq\unions{K}$.
    \begin{subproof}
        Fix $i\in\unions{K}$.
        Take $J\in K$ such that $i\in J$ by \cref{unions_iff}.
        $\downward{R}{i}\subseteq J$ by \cref{common_initial_segment}.
        $J\subseteq\unions{K}$ by \cref{subseteq,unions_iff}.
        Follows by \cref{subseteq_transitive}.
    \end{subproof}

    Show that for all $i\in\unions{K}$ we have $\downward{S}{i}\subseteq\unions{K}$.
    \begin{subproof}
        Fix $i\in\unions{K}$.
        Take $J\in K$ such that $i\in J$ by \cref{unions_iff}.
        $\downward{S}{i}\subseteq J$ by \cref{common_initial_segment}.
        $J\subseteq\unions{K}$ by \cref{subseteq,unions_iff}.
        Follows by \cref{subseteq_transitive}.
    \end{subproof}

    Follows by \cref{common_initial_segment}.
\end{proof}

% from §13 helper lemma 3: compatibility of generated well orderings
\begin{lemma}\label{generated_well_orderings_compatible}
    Suppose $R$ is a well ordering generated by $f$ on $B$.
    Suppose $S$ is a well ordering generated by $f$ on $C$.
    Then $B$ is a common initial segment of $R$ on $B$ and $S$ on $C$
    or $C$ is a common initial segment of $R$ on $B$ and $S$ on $C$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    Let $K = \{I\in\pow{B\inter C}\mid \text{$I$ is a common initial segment of $R$ on $B$ and $S$ on $C$}\}$.
    $\emptyset\in K$ by \cref{powerset_bottom,empty_common_initial_segment}.
    Thus $K$ is inhabited.
    Every element of $K$ is a common initial segment of $R$ on $B$ and $S$ on $C$.
    Let $I = \unions{K}$.
    $I$ is a common initial segment of $R$ on $B$ and $S$ on $C$
        by \cref{union_common_initial_segments}.

    Show $B\subseteq I$ or $C\subseteq I$.
    \begin{subproof}
        Suppose not.
        $B\setminus I\neq\emptyset$ and $C\setminus I\neq\emptyset$
            by \cref{setminus_eq_emptyset_iff_subseteq}.
        Take $b\in B\setminus I$ such that for all $x\in B\setminus I$
            we have $b\mathrel{R}x$ or $b=x$ by \cref{generated_well_ordering,foundational_ordering,setminus_subseteq}.
        Take $c\in C\setminus I$ such that for all $x\in C\setminus I$
            we have $c\mathrel{S}x$ or $c=x$ by \cref{generated_well_ordering,foundational_ordering,setminus_subseteq}.

        Show $\downward{R}{b}=I$.
        \begin{subproof}
            Every element of $\downward{R}{b}$ is an element of $I$
                by \cref{generated_well_ordering,foundational_ordering,subseteq,times_tuple_elim,downward_closure,setminus,asymmetric}.
            Show that every element of $I$ is an element of $\downward{R}{b}$.
            \begin{subproof}
                Fix $x\in I$.
                $x,b\in B$ and $x\neq b$ by \cref{common_initial_segment,inter_elim_left,subseteq,setminus}.
                $\downward{R}{x}\subseteq I$ by \cref{common_initial_segment}.
                Thus $x\mathrel{R}b$ by \cref{generated_well_ordering,foundational_ordering,connex,dom_intro,downward_closure,subseteq,setminus}.
                Follows by \cref{dom_intro,downward_closure}.
            \end{subproof}
            Follows by set extensionality.
        \end{subproof}

        Show $\downward{S}{c}=I$.
        \begin{subproof}
            Every element of $\downward{S}{c}$ is an element of $I$
                by \cref{generated_well_ordering,foundational_ordering,subseteq,times_tuple_elim,downward_closure,setminus,asymmetric}.
            Show that every element of $I$ is an element of $\downward{S}{c}$.
            \begin{subproof}
                Fix $x\in I$.
                $x,c\in C$ and $x\neq c$ by \cref{common_initial_segment,inter_elim_right,subseteq,setminus}.
                $\downward{S}{x}\subseteq I$ by \cref{common_initial_segment}.
                Thus $x\mathrel{S}c$ by \cref{generated_well_ordering,foundational_ordering,connex,dom_intro,downward_closure,subseteq,setminus}.
                Follows by \cref{dom_intro,downward_closure}.
            \end{subproof}
            Follows by set extensionality.
        \end{subproof}

        $f(I)=b$ and $f(I)=c$ by \cref{generated_well_ordering,setminus}.
        Thus $b=c$.
        Let $J=\cons{b}{I}$.
        $J\subseteq B\inter C$ by \cref{common_initial_segment,setminus,inter_intro,cons_subseteq_intro}.

        Show that for all $j\in J$ we have $\downward{R}{j}=\downward{S}{j}$.
        \begin{subproof}
            Fix $j\in J$.
            Follows by \cref{cons_iff,common_initial_segment}.
        \end{subproof}

        Show that for all $j\in J$ we have $\downward{R}{j}\subseteq J$.
        \begin{subproof}
            Fix $j\in J$.
            Follows by \cref{cons_iff,common_initial_segment,subseteq_cons_self,subseteq_transitive}.
        \end{subproof}

        Show that for all $j\in J$ we have $\downward{S}{j}\subseteq J$.
        \begin{subproof}
            Fix $j\in J$.
            Follows by \cref{cons_iff,common_initial_segment,subseteq_cons_self,subseteq_transitive}.
        \end{subproof}

        Thus $J$ is a common initial segment of $R$ on $B$ and $S$ on $C$
            by \cref{common_initial_segment}.
        Hence $J\in K$ by \cref{pow_iff}.
        Therefore $b\in I$ by \cref{cons_left,unions_iff}.
        Contradiction by \cref{setminus}.
    \end{subproof}

    \begin{byCase}
    \caseOf{$B\subseteq I$.}
        $I\subseteq B\inter C$ by \cref{common_initial_segment}.
        Thus $I=B$ and $B\subseteq C$ by \cref{inter_elim_left,inter_elim_right,subseteq,subseteq_antisymmetric}.
        Thus $B$ is a common initial segment of $R$ on $B$ and $S$ on $C$.
        Follows by assumption.
    \caseOf{$C\subseteq I$.}
        $I\subseteq B\inter C$ by \cref{common_initial_segment}.
        Thus $I=C$ and $C\subseteq B$ by \cref{inter_elim_left,inter_elim_right,subseteq,subseteq_antisymmetric}.
        Thus $C$ is a common initial segment of $R$ on $B$ and $S$ on $C$.
        Follows by assumption.
    \end{byCase}
\end{proof}

% from §13 helper lemma 4: the empty generated well ordering
\begin{lemma}\label{empty_generated_well_ordering}
    $\emptyset$ is a well ordering generated by $f$ on $\emptyset$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    $\emptyset$ is transitive by \cref{transitive,emptyset}.
    $\emptyset$ is asymmetric by \cref{asymmetric,emptyset}.
    $\emptyset$ is connex on $\emptyset$ by \cref{connex,emptyset}.
    $\emptyset$ is a foundational ordering on $\emptyset$
        by \cref{foundational_ordering,emptyset,emptyset_subseteq,subseteq_emptyset_iff}.
    Follows by \cref{generated_well_ordering,emptyset}.
\end{proof}

% from §13 helper lemma 5: adjoining a generated last element
\begin{lemma}\label{generated_well_ordering_adjoin}
    Suppose $R$ is a well ordering generated by $f$ on $B$.
    Suppose $c\notin B$ and $f(B)=c$.
    Suppose $S=R\union(B\times\{c\})$.
    Then $S$ is a well ordering generated by $f$ on $\cons{c}{B}$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    Let $C=\cons{c}{B}$.
    $R\subseteq B\times B$ by \cref{generated_well_ordering,foundational_ordering}.
    $B\subseteq C$ and $c\in C$ by \cref{subseteq_cons_self,cons_left}.
    Thus $B\times B\subseteq C\times C$ by \cref{times_subseteq_left,times_subseteq_right,subseteq_transitive}.
    Hence $R\subseteq C\times C$ by \cref{subseteq_transitive}.
    $\{c\}\subseteq C$ by \cref{singleton_subset_intro}.
    Thus $B\times\{c\}\subseteq C\times C$ by \cref{times_subseteq_left,times_subseteq_right,subseteq_transitive}.
    Hence $S\subseteq C\times C$ by \cref{union_subsets_is_subset}.
    Show $S$ is transitive.
    \begin{subproof}
        It suffices to show that for all $x,y,z$ such that $x\mathrel{S}y\mathrel{S}z$
            we have $x\mathrel{S}z$ by \cref{transitive}.
        Fix $x$.
        Fix $y$.
        Fix $z$.
        Assume $x\mathrel{S}y\mathrel{S}z$.
        Show $x\mathrel{R}y$.
        \begin{subproof}
            Suppose not.
            $(x,y)\in B\times\{c\}$ by \cref{union_iff}.
            Thus $y=c$ by \cref{times_tuple_elim,singleton_iff}.
